========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yes, thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess I came in because... I’ve started doing things lately that honestly really scare me. I'm a computer systems analyst, and I've always been a very logical, structured person, you know? I have my routines, I got my bachelor's, I've always been very dependable. But lately... well, for instance, I've been walking out in the middle of major software deployments. And if you know anything about IT, a deployment is a huge deal. You have to be there, monitoring everything, making sure the systems don't crash during the transition, and I just... I just got up and left. Didn't tell my team, didn't hand off my tasks, just walked out the door while everything was live. 

And it’s not just at work, it’s happening at home too. I’ll just grab my keys at two or three in the morning and start driving around late at night with absolutely no plan. Just driving out on the empty roads for hours, not really caring where I end up or how fast I'm going. And I sit there behind the wheel thinking, what am I doing? Because this is just not how I’ve ever handled stress before. Ever. I’ve always been the reliable one, the one who fixes the problems, not the one who just abandons a critical server update or wanders the highways in the pitch black. It's like I can't stop myself from doing these reckless things, and I really don't understand it. I keep trying to figure out what's triggering it, but I just end up doing it again, which is why I finally made this appointment, because I just... I can't keep living like this.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Down? Yeah. I mean, I guess that ties into feeling so drained, but it's more than just being tired. It's like... I've completely lost the thread of what I actually enjoy doing. 

I used to be so structured with my free time, just like I am at work. I would literally plan my entire week around these activities I loved. Tuesday nights were always salsa dancing—I've been going to the same studio for years, and it was such a great release after staring at code all day. And then Thursday was my book club, and Saturday mornings I was absolutely religious about my yoga class. I even loved hosting. I'd have people over on the weekends and spend hours, literally hours, cooking traditional Guatemalan food for everyone. It takes so much prep work, you know, roasting the tomatoes and chilies, getting all the spices right, but I loved it. I loved the noise and the people and just... being engaged in life.

But now? Now I just... I don't even know. I mostly just cancel everything. I'll get a text from the book club girls, or an invite to go dancing, and I'll just stare at my phone and think of some excuse. I just can't bring myself to go. The thought of grocery shopping and cooking and pretending to be happy and engaged for a dinner party just feels like climbing a mountain right now. 

So instead, I just sit at home. And it's not like I'm doing anything productive or relaxing with that time either. I just sit on the couch, mindlessly scrolling through my phone for hours, not even registering what I'm looking at. Or I'll turn on the TV and just... pretend to watch a show. Like, the colors are flashing on the screen, and the sound is on, but I'm not following the plot at all. I'm just sort of existing in the room, letting the time pass until it's late enough to go to sleep, or until I get that weird urge to go drive around in the dark. It's really sad when I stop and think about it, because I miss caring about things. I just don't have it in me right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I mean, my weight has always been pretty stable. I’m a size eight, have been since my mid-thirties, and all my work slacks still fit exactly the same. I was actually just noticing that the other morning when I was getting dressed for work—I was putting on this navy blue suit I bought at Nordstrom maybe three years ago, and the waistband is exactly as it always is. So, definitely no weight loss. 

And as for my appetite... well, it’s funny you ask, because like I said, I’m not cooking those big traditional Guatemalan meals anymore. I just don't have the energy to roast the pepitas or make the recado from scratch. It's just too much standing around and measuring. But I am still eating. I’ve just transitioned to... well, mostly takeout or things I don't have to think about. There’s this Thai place down the street from my house, and I’ve probably ordered their Pad Kee Mao three times this week alone. I just open the delivery app, click reorder, and it shows up at my door. And I'll eat the whole thing while I’m sitting there staring blankly at the TV. Or I’ll just make a quick sandwich, just turkey and provolone on whole wheat, nothing special. 

So the quality of my food has definitely gone down, and I’m certainly not getting the joy out of it that I used to when I was cooking for friends, but the actual amount I’m eating? No, that hasn't changed at all. I still get hungry right around noon for lunch, like clockwork, and then I eat dinner around seven. So, yeah, no changes there. I guess my body is just running on autopilot when it comes to food, even if the rest of my life feels completely off track.

Oh, no. No, never anything like that. I mean, I know I was just going on and on about how heavy everything feels and how I just sit around doing absolutely nothing, which, believe me, is so incredibly out of character for me. So even though I feel completely broken right now, and I can't figure out why I'm abandoning projects or driving around in the middle of the night feeling empty, I still want to fix the bug. I want to troubleshoot this. I would never actually do anything to hurt myself or permanently end things. I'm just... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of the time? I mean... it's hard to say exactly. I wouldn't say I feel actively depressed every single minute of the day. Like, when I'm at work, even though I'm struggling with the bigger projects, I can still get through the day-to-day stuff. If someone asks me to review a piece of code or check a system log, I can do it, and in those moments, I'm just focused on the task, you know? I'm not sitting there crying at my desk or anything. 

But it's this underlying feeling... it's like a low-level hum that's always there in the background. Like when a server is running hot and you can hear the fan going constantly—you might tune it out for a while when you're busy, but the second the room gets quiet, you hear it again, and you know something isn't right. That's how this feels. So maybe I'm not actively feeling "down" while I'm in a meeting, but as soon as the meeting ends, or as soon as I log off for the day, that heaviness just crashes back down on me. 

And then the weekends... the weekends are the worst, because there's no structure to distract me. Like I was saying before, I used to fill my weekends with yoga and friends and cooking, and now it's just this vast, empty stretch of time. And during those hours, sitting on the couch, staring at the TV, yeah, I definitely feel down. It's this overwhelming sense of... blah. Just completely flat and drained. So, is it most of the time? I guess if you add up all the hours where I'm not actively distracted by work, then yeah, it probably is most of the time. It just sort of colors everything, even when I'm trying not to think about it.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it is nearly every day. The low mood, like I said, it’s always humming in the background, but the lack of interest... that's the part that really throws me, and it's definitely an everyday thing now. It doesn't matter if it's a Tuesday evening or a Saturday afternoon, and it certainly doesn't require a stressful event to trigger it. It's just... a constant state of not caring anymore. 

And that's so incredibly bizarre for me, because I have always been such an active, scheduled person. I mean, my Google Calendar used to look like a Tetris board. I would literally plan my entire week down to the hour. Monday nights were for salsa dancing—I went to this great studio downtown, and I’d been going for years, so I knew all the instructors and half the regulars. Wednesdays were my book club, which I actually started with a few women from my old neighborhood, and we took it very seriously, reading everything from historical fiction to heavy non-fiction. And then I had my Vinyasa yoga classes on Thursday evenings and Saturday mornings... and, of course, the weekends were for having people over. I used to love hosting. I’d spend all Saturday morning at the specialty markets getting the right ingredients for my grandmother's Guatemalan recipes, making huge batches of pepián or tamales colorados, and having six or eight people over for dinner. 

But now? Now, I just... I can't. I literally cannot muster the energy or the desire to do any of it. The salsa studio sends me emails about new sessions, and I just delete them. The book club text thread goes off, and I just mute it. I haven't been to yoga in months. I mostly just cancel whatever plans I tentatively make, or I just don't make them at all. And instead of doing all those things I used to love, I just sit at home. I'll sit on the sofa in my living room, and I'll just scroll through my phone for hours, not even really looking at anything, just swiping. Or I'll turn on the TV and put on some show, but I'm just pretending to watch it, really. The screen is flashing, but I couldn't tell you the plot or the characters' names. I'm just... existing in the room. And it happens every single day, just this total, complete absence of wanting to participate in my own life.

I’ll just grab my keys at two or three in the morning and start driving around late at night with absolutely no plan. When you're suddenly doing things that make absolutely no sense, like abandoning a critical server migration—which, by the way, took months of planning and coordination with three different departments just to schedule—your confidence just plummets. I've spent my whole career building this reputation as someone who is rock-solid, and now I feel like I can't even trust my own brain. So, definitely no overconfidence. \n\nAnd as for being unusually energetic or doing things quickly... gosh, I wish. I feel so drained most of the time. Even when I do those late-night drives I mentioned, it's not because I'm wired or feeling this intense rush of energy. I'm not speeding or blasting music or feeling invincible. I'm just sitting there in the dark, gripping the steering wheel, feeling completely exhausted and hollow. Everything just feels like it takes so much more effort lately. Like, even just looking at a system log or reviewing a simple line of code feels incredibly heavy and slow. So, no, I haven't had any periods where I felt sped up or unusually irritable or energetic. It's really just this constant, heavy confusion about why I'm acting this way, and it just takes all the energy out of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? I would literally plan my entire week around these activities I loved. Tuesday nights were always salsa dancing—I've been going to the same studio for years, and it was such a great release after staring at code all day. And then Thursday was my book club, and Saturday mornings I was absolutely religious about my yoga class. I even loved hosting. I'd have people over on the weekends and spend hours, literally hours, cooking traditional Guatemalan food for everyone. It takes so much prep work, you know, roasting the tomatoes and chilies, getting all the spices right, but I loved it. I loved the noise and the people and just... being engaged in life.\n\nBut now? Now I just... I don't even know. I mostly just cancel everything. I'll get a text from the book club girls, or an invite to go dancing, and I'll just stare at my phone and think of some excuse. I just can't bring myself to go. The thought of grocery shopping and cooking and pretending to be happy and engaged for a dinner party just feels like climbing a mountain right now. \n\nSo instead, I just sit at home. I'm just sort of existing in the room, letting the time pass until it's late enough to go to sleep, or until I get that weird urge to go drive around in the dark. It's really sad when I stop and think about it, because I miss caring about things. I just don't have it in me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? Like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, my weight has always been pretty stable. I’m a size eight, have been since my mid-thirties, and all my work slacks still fit exactly the same. I was actually just noticing that the other morning when I was getting dressed for work—I was putting on this navy blue suit I bought at Nordstrom maybe three years ago, and the waistband is exactly as it always is. So, definitely no weight loss. \n\nAnd as for my appetite... well, it’s funny you ask, because like I said, I’m not cooking those big traditional Guatemalan meals anymore. I just don't have the energy to roast the pepitas or make the recado from scratch. It's just too much standing around and measuring. But I am still eating. I’ve just transitioned to... well, mostly takeout or things I don't have to think about. There’s this Thai place down the street from my house, and I’ve probably ordered their Pad Kee Mao three times this week alone. I just open the delivery app, click reorder, and it shows up at my door. And I'll eat the whole thing while I’m sitting there staring blankly at the TV. Or I’ll just make a quick sandwich, just turkey and provolone on whole wheat, nothing special. \n\nSo the quality of my food has definitely gone down, and I’m certainly not getting the joy out of it that I used to when I was cooking for friends, but the actual amount I’m eating? No, that hasn't changed at all. I still get hungry right around noon for lunch, like clockwork, and then I eat dinner around seven. So, yeah, no changes there. I guess my body is just running on autopilot when it comes to food, even if the rest of my life feels completely off track.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, never anything like that. I mean, I know I was just going on and on about how heavy everything feels and how I just sit around doing absolutely nothing, which, believe me, is so incredibly out of character for me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Slowed down? Like, physically moving in slow motion? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know I just spent all that time explaining how I’m sitting on the couch for hours on end, doing absolutely nothing, but when I actually do get up, I’m moving at my normal pace. Just yesterday, for example, I had to go downstairs to get the mail because the carrier left a package—some ethernet cables I ordered weeks ago and completely forgot about, honestly, because who even cares about home networking projects right now—and I walked down my building's stairs just as fast as I usually do. I didn't feel like I was wading through molasses or anything. And at work, when I do have to walk over to the server room or go to the breakroom to get my coffee, I'm still walking at that same brisk pace I always have. I've always been a fast walker. Growing up, my dad always walked fast, and you just had to keep up, so I definitely haven't lost that physical momentum when I'm actually on my feet. 

And as for my thoughts... no, they don't feel slow. It's not like my brain is running on a dial-up connection, if that makes sense. It's more like... the bandwidth is just being taken up by static. So I might be staring at a screen, totally checked out and not doing what I'm supposed to be doing, but my brain isn't operating slowly. It's just not engaging. I can still process information quickly if I absolutely have to. Like, if there's a critical error on a system I'm monitoring, I can read the logs and spot the anomaly just as fast as I could a year ago. My typing speed is still well over ninety words a minute—I actually took one of those online typing tests the other day just out of curiosity, probably just procrastinating on a real assignment, but my fingers still fly across the keyboard when I actually have something to type. 

So, no, the processing speed is completely fine. The hardware is fine. It's just the desire to actually run the programs that's completely missing. So I wouldn't say I'm moving or thinking slowly at all. It's just that I'm choosing—well, not choosing, but I end up—just staying completely still instead. But when I move, I move normally.

I just want my old life back, you know? I want to be the person who could handle a massive system rollout without a second thought, the one who actually looked forward to a busy weekend. I think, because of what I do for a living—analyzing systems, finding bugs, fixing broken processes—my brain just doesn't go to \"destroy the whole machine\" when something isn't working right. You don't just scrap a whole network because of a glitch, even a really bad one. \n\nSo even though I feel completely broken right now, and I can't figure out why I'm abandoning projects or driving around in the middle of the night feeling empty, I still want to fix the bug. I want to troubleshoot this. I would never actually do anything to hurt myself or permanently end things. I'm just... I'm really tired, and I'm so confused about why my brain is acting this way, but I definitely still want to be here to figure it out and get back to normal. So, no, absolutely no thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed most of the time, not just occasionally?\n\nPatient: Most of the time? I mean... it's hard to say exactly. I wouldn't say I feel actively depressed every single minute of the day. Like, when I'm at work, even though I'm struggling with the bigger projects, I can still get through the day-to-day stuff. If someone asks me to review a piece of code or check a system log, I can do it, and in those moments, I'm just focused on the task, you know? I'm not sitting there crying at my desk or anything. \n\nBut it's this underlying feeling... it's like a low-level hum that's always there in the background. Like when a server is running hot and you can hear the fan going constantly—you might tune it out for a while when you're busy, but the second the room gets quiet, you hear it again, and you know something isn't right. That's how this feels. So maybe I'm not actively feeling \"down\" while I'm in a meeting, but as soon as the meeting ends, or as soon as I log off for the day, that heaviness just crashes back down on me. \n\nAnd then the weekends... the weekends are the worst, because there's no structure to distract me. Like I was saying before, I used to fill my weekends with yoga and friends and cooking, and now it's just this vast, empty stretch of time. And during those hours, sitting on the couch, staring at the TV, yeah, I definitely feel down. It's this overwhelming sense of... blah. Just completely flat and drained. So, is it most of the time? I guess if you add up all the hours where I'm not actively distracted by work, then yeah, it probably is most of the time. It just sort of colors everything, even when I'm trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and lack of interest in things happen nearly every day, and last for most of the day, even when nothing stressful is happening?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is nearly every day. The low mood, like I said, it’s always humming in the background, but the lack of interest... that's the part that really throws me, and it's definitely an everyday thing now. It doesn't matter if it's a Tuesday evening or a Saturday afternoon, and it certainly doesn't require a stressful event to trigger it. It's just... a constant state of not caring anymore. \n\nAnd that's so incredibly bizarre for me, because I have always been such an active, scheduled person. I mean, my Google Calendar used to look like a Tetris board. I would literally plan my entire week down to the hour. Monday nights were for salsa dancing—I went to this great studio downtown, and I’d been going for years, so I knew all the instructors and half the regulars. Wednesdays were my book club, which I actually started with a few women from my old neighborhood, and we took it very seriously, reading everything from historical fiction to heavy non-fiction. And then I had my Vinyasa yoga classes on Thursday evenings and Saturday mornings... and, of course, the weekends were for having people over. I used to love hosting. I’d spend all Saturday morning at the specialty markets getting the right ingredients for my grandmother's Guatemalan recipes, making huge batches of pepián or tamales colorados, and having six or eight people over for dinner. \n\nBut now? Now, I just... I can't. I literally cannot muster the energy or the desire to do any of it. The salsa studio sends me emails about new sessions, and I just delete them. The book club text thread goes off, and I just mute it. I haven't been to yoga in months. I mostly just cancel whatever plans I tentatively make, or I just don't make them at all. And instead of doing all those things I used to love, I just sit at home. I'll sit on the sofa in my living room, and I'll just scroll through my phone for hours, not even really looking at anything, just swiping. Or I'll turn on the TV and put on some show, but I'm just pretending to watch it, really. The screen is flashing, but I couldn't tell you the plot or the characters' names. I'm just... existing in the room. And it happens every single day, just this total, complete absence of wanting to participate in my own life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you’re moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Like, physically moving in slow motion? No, I don't think so. Just yesterday, for example, I had to go downstairs to get the mail because the carrier left a package—some ethernet cables I ordered weeks ago and completely forgot about, honestly, because who even cares about home networking projects right now—and I walked down my building's stairs just as fast as I usually do. I didn't feel like I was wading through molasses or anything. And at work, when I do have to walk over to the server room or go to the breakroom to get my coffee, I'm still walking at that same brisk pace I always have. I've always been a fast walker. Growing up, my dad always walked fast, and you just had to keep up, so I definitely haven't lost that physical momentum when I'm actually on my feet. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts... no, they don't feel slow. It's just... a constant state of not caring anymore. \n\nAnd that's so incredibly bizarre for me, because I have always been such an active, scheduled person. And instead of doing all those things I used to love, I just sit at home. I'll sit on the sofa in my living room, and I'll just scroll through my phone for hours, not even really looking at anything, just swiping. I'm just... existing in the room. Just yesterday, for example, I had to go downstairs to get the mail because the carrier left a package—some ethernet cables I ordered weeks ago and completely forgot about, honestly, because who even cares about home networking projects right now—and I walked down my building's stairs just as fast as I usually do. I didn't feel like I was wading through molasses or anything. And at work, when I do have to walk over to the server room or go to the breakroom to get my coffee, I'm still walking at that same brisk pace I always have. I've always been a fast walker. Growing up, my dad always walked fast, and you just had to keep up, so I definitely haven't lost that physical momentum when I'm actually on my feet. \n\nAnd as for my thoughts... no, they don't feel slow. It's not like my brain is running on a dial-up connection, if that makes sense. It's more like... the bandwidth is just being taken up by static. So I might be staring at a screen, totally checked out and not doing what I'm supposed to be doing, but my brain isn't operating slowly. It's just not engaging. I can still process information quickly if I absolutely have to. Like, if there's a critical error on a system I'm monitoring, I can read the logs and spot the anomaly just as fast as I could a year ago. My typing speed is still well over ninety words a minute—I actually took one of those online typing tests the other day just out of curiosity, probably just procrastinating on a real assignment, but my fingers still fly across the keyboard when I actually have something to type. \n\nSo, no, the processing speed is completely fine. It's just that I'm choosing—well, not choosing, but I end up—just staying completely still instead. Fatigued? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It’s actually kind of a weird distinction to make, but I’ve thought about it a lot. Because, you know, when you tell people you spend your entire weekend staring at a wall or mindlessly scrolling on the couch, their first assumption is always, 'Oh, you must be exhausted.' But I'm really not. It's not a physical lack of energy at all. 

Like I said before about not moving slowly—my body feels completely fine. If I needed to sprint to catch a bus, I absolutely have the physical energy to do it. It's more like... well, working in IT, the best way I can describe it is like having a server rack that is fully powered on, plugged into an uninterruptible power supply, drawing the perfect wattage, but there's just no traffic being routed to it. The power is there. The energy is there. I'll get a full night's rest, wake up, and my body is completely rested. I don't have heavy limbs, I don't feel that bone-deep exhaustion you get when you've been working back-to-back double shifts. 

I mean, I remember back when I was finishing my bachelor's degree while working full-time on a helpdesk—now *that* was fatigue. I would literally fall asleep on my textbooks at the kitchen table, just completely drained of all physical resources. But this? This is entirely different. I'm awake, I have the physical capacity to do things, my energy reserves are completely normal. I just... I don't use them. I just sit there. So, no, I wouldn't say I've lost any energy or feel fatigued. It's just the absolute lack of desire to apply the energy I *do* have to anything at all.

I don't have heavy limbs, I don't feel that bone-deep exhaustion you get when you've been working back-to-back double shifts. \n\nI mean, I remember back when I was finishing my bachelor's degree while working full-time on a helpdesk—now *that* was fatigue. I would literally fall asleep on my textbooks at the kitchen table, just completely drained of all physical resources. But this? This is entirely different. I'm awake, I have the physical capacity to do things, my energy reserves are completely normal. I just... I don't use them. I just sit there. So, no, I wouldn't say I've lost any energy or feel fatigued. It's just the absolute lack of desire to apply the energy I *do* have to anything at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Restless? No... no, definitely not. It's actually, um, it's quite the opposite, really. Like I was saying earlier about sitting on the couch for hours—I really mean I am just *sitting* there. I don't fidget, I don't bounce my leg, I don't pace around the living room. It's not like I have this pent-up nervous energy that I need to burn off or anything like that.

I've never really been like that, and I'm certainly not like that now. If anything, I am entirely too still. 

I can get into a spot on the couch, or in my ergonomic chair at my desk, and I will just freeze there. Sometimes I'll realize that three or four hours have gone by and I haven't so much as shifted my weight. I won't even realize my leg has completely fallen asleep until I finally force myself to get up to use the restroom or get water, and then I'm standing there with terrible pins and needles because I was just completely, 100 percent motionless for half the afternoon. 

So, no, there’s no pacing, no agitation. I don't have that restless feeling at all. My mind might be checked out and unengaged, like I said before, but my body is just... planted. It's just completely anchored to wherever I happen to sit down. I don't feel any urge to get up and move around for no reason, so I definitely wouldn't say I'm restless.

Low mood? I mean... I wouldn't really call it a low mood, honestly. It's not like I'm walking around crying or feeling sad all day. That's what people usually think of when they say 'low mood,' right? Like you're grieving or you've had your heart broken or something. But it's not like that at all for me. 

It's more just... flat. Like I was saying before about the server rack—it's just powered on with no traffic. I don't feel sad, I don't feel happy, I just kind of feel... nothing. I wake up, and I just exist. There's no real emotional response to anything. If a project at work goes well, I don't feel relieved or proud. If a client yells at me, I don't feel stressed or upset. It's just a completely neutral, gray baseline that never really shifts. 

So, I wouldn't say I have a low mood, per se. It's not this heavy, oppressive sadness that I'm carrying around. It's just a complete absence of feeling. It's like my emotional bandwidth has been throttled down to zero, and nothing gets through, good or bad. I don't know if that makes sense, but it's definitely not what I would describe as a low mood. It's just... a void, I guess. So, no, I wouldn't say I've been experiencing a low mood most of the day, because I really haven't been experiencing much of a mood at all.
